Not even like I needed coffee, which is saying something because I usually get one before work—there’s this place near the office that always messes up my order—but anyway, yeah, I’d be up and going like I’d slept a full night.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only three or four hours and still felt charged, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Earlier this month I felt almost, like, lit up? Like I could handle anything, and I was just moving through the day feeling unstoppable. Not exactly happy like “everything is wonderful,” but really up.\n\nBut then it would flip, too. Like my mom would ask me some normal question—she does this thing where she asks if I paid a bill even though I literally do bookkeeping all day—and I’d snap at her. Or my supervisor would ask something small and I’d get irritated way faster than I normally would. So… yes, up, but also really touchy.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Like, more than just a good mood. I started thinking I could, I don’t know, pay down my condo really fast, finally pass the CPA exams, and run a little bookkeeping business on the side all at once. Like failure just wasn’t even on the table.\n\nWhich sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because I have a stack of mail on my counter I keep meaning to sort, and my printer at home doesn’t even work half the time. I even started sketching out, like, how I’d find clients… which is funny because my kitchen table was covered in random papers and receipts while I’m sitting there planning a whole extra business. It was like I couldn’t shut it off once I started.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, were you more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I can’t even get a word in.”\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, like I was pushing the words out because I had to keep going before I lost the thread. Not completely like I can’t function, no… I’m still getting to work. But it’s definitely been affecting things. I’ve made enough reconciliation mistakes that my supervisor pulled me aside, which was embarrassing because that’s kind of my whole job, you know?

Not even like I needed coffee, which is saying something because I usually get one before work—there’s this place near the office that always messes up my order—but anyway, yeah, I’d be up and going like I’d slept a full night.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only three or four hours and still felt charged, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Earlier this month I felt almost, like, lit up? Like I could handle anything, and I was just moving through the day feeling unstoppable. Not exactly happy like “everything is wonderful,” but really up.\n\nBut then it would flip, too. Like my mom would ask me some normal question—she does this thing where she asks if I paid a bill even though I literally do bookkeeping all day—and I’d snap at her. Or my supervisor would ask something small and I’d get irritated way faster than I normally would. So… yes, up, but also really touchy.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Like, more than just a good mood. I started thinking I could, I don’t know, pay down my condo really fast, finally pass the CPA exams, and run a little bookkeeping business on the side all at once. Like failure just wasn’t even on the table.\n\nWhich sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because I have a stack of mail on my counter I keep meaning to sort, and my printer at home doesn’t even work half the time. But in those moments I really believed I could just handle all of it.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did you find yourself taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’d get home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’d start cleaning, then making these big renovation lists for my condo, like pricing out cabinets and floors even though I wasn’t actually ready to do any of it.\n\nAnd then I’d be checking interest rates online, like obsessively comparing them, and writing down ideas for side bookkeeping jobs I could pick up. I even started sketching out, like, how I’d find clients… which is funny because my kitchen table was covered in random papers and receipts while I’m sitting there planning a whole extra business. It was like I couldn’t shut it off once I started.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, were you more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My sister actually pointed it out before I did. I was going on and on about, like, home equity and taxes and then somehow paint colors for the condo—because I was thinking about renovating, but also the tax stuff tied into work, sort of—and she was like, “Can you slow down? I can’t even get a word in.”\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, like I was pushing the words out because I had to keep going before I lost the thread. It wasn’t just chatting more, it was like… I couldn’t pause.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. It was like my brain kept flipping channels—mortgage payments, then CPA study plans, then whether I should redo the kitchen cabinets, and then back to money again.\n\nAnd I’d be lying in bed trying to relax, but I couldn’t really slow it down. Like I’d tell myself, okay, just pick one thing, but then another thought would jump in before I could even finish the first one.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, did they ever cause problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still getting to work and everything, so it’s not like I just stopped showing up. But I’ve made some reconciliation mistakes—enough that my supervisor pulled me aside, which was really embarrassing because numbers are supposed to be the thing I’m good at.\n\nAnd with people, it’s more like I kind of disappear. Like, I was doing salsa class for a while—my friend talked me into it, and I’m not even that coordinated, but it was fun—and lately during those periods I’ve been avoiding that and not really seeing friends. I just don’t feel like myself, and then explaining that feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: When these unusually up or irritable periods happen, do they last for at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I think so, yeah, but it’s kind of been messy to track. The really obvious back-and-forth, like being all charged up and then crashing, has been happening for about a month now—basically since the mortgage became real.\n\nI keep tying it to that because everything got so… official, with the paperwork and numbers and thinking about payments, and then I’d be up in my head about it for stretches. So, yes, it feels like more than just a day or two, but I’d have to look back to say exactly how many days in a row.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve never been hospitalized for it. The closest thing is just feeling like everything is getting away from me, but I haven’t gone to a hospital or had anyone take me in or anything like that.\n\nHospitals make me think of when my cousin had her baby and the parking was a nightmare—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But no, not for those up periods.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of those up periods, do other people notice that you’re acting differently than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sister noticed. She said I was talking really fast—like I was jumping from home equity to taxes to paint colors, all in one breath—and she couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nI remember feeling it too, like I knew I was kind of steamrolling her, but I also felt this pressure to keep going because if I stopped I’d lose the thread. Which is funny because she’s usually the one who talks a lot, especially about her kitchen remodel—sorry, not the point. But yes, she definitely noticed I wasn’t talking like my usual self.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, did the symptoms ever get so severe that you couldn’t function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Not completely like I can’t function, no… I’m still getting to work. But it’s definitely been affecting things. I’ve made enough reconciliation mistakes that my supervisor pulled me aside, which was embarrassing because that’s kind of my whole job, you know?\n\nAnd at home or just life stuff, I haven’t really been keeping up socially. I’ve been avoiding salsa class and my friends because I don’t feel like myself. Salsa used to be the one thing where I could just go and not think about spreadsheets or mortgage numbers—well, except there’s always someone there talking about buying a condo lately, which somehow follows me everywhere. But yeah, I’m functioning, just not well.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had a period lasting at least 2 weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Lately I wake up and it’s like this heavy, empty feeling is already there before I even get out of bed. Some nights I’m up half the night, like my brain won’t shut off, just jumping from one thing to another. And then other times I wake up way too early and it feels like I didn’t really sleep at all.\n\nI mean, I know everyone says they’re tired—my coworker says that all the time during tax season, and she drinks those huge iced coffees—but this feels different. Like I’m going through the day not rested, and it’s starting to really bother me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energized or up, even while sleeping very little?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, there have been times like that. Like, I’d only sleep maybe three or four hours, which normally would make me useless the next day, but I’d wake up feeling weirdly charged. Not even like I needed coffee, which is saying something because I usually get one before work—there’s this place near the office that always messes up my order—but anyway, yeah, I’d be up and going like I’d slept a full night.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only three or four hours and still felt charged, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Earlier this month I felt almost, like, lit up? Like I could handle anything, and I was just moving through the day feeling unstoppable. Not exactly happy like “everything is wonderful,” but really up.\n\nBut then it would flip, too. Like my mom would ask me some normal question—she does this thing where she asks if I paid a bill even though I literally do bookkeeping all day—and I’d snap at her. Or my supervisor would ask something small and I’d get irritated way faster than I normally would. So… yes, up, but also really touchy.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did you feel more confident or powerful than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Like, more than just a good mood. I started thinking I could, I don’t know, pay down my condo really fast, finally pass the CPA exams, and run a little bookkeeping business on the side all at once. Like failure just wasn’t even on the table.\n\nWhich sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because I have a stack of mail on my counter I keep meaning to sort, and my printer at home doesn’t even work half the time. But in those moments I really believed I could just handle all of it.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did you find yourself taking on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I’d get home from work and instead of just making dinner or watching something, I’d start cleaning, then making these big renovation lists for my condo, like pricing out cabinets and floors even though I wasn’t actually ready to do any of it.\n\nAnd then I’d be checking interest rates online, like obsessively comparing them, and writing down ideas for side bookkeeping jobs I could pick up. I even started sketching out, like, how I’d find clients… which is funny because my kitchen table was covered in random papers and receipts while I’m sitting there planning a whole extra business. It was like I couldn’t shut it off once I started.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, were you more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My sister actually pointed it out before I did. I was going on and on about, like, home equity and taxes and then somehow paint colors for the condo—because I was thinking about renovating, but also the tax stuff tied into work, sort of—and she was like, “Can you slow down? I can’t even get a word in.”\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, like I was pushing the words out because I had to keep going before I lost the thread. It wasn’t just chatting more, it was like… I couldn’t pause.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually up, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. It was like my brain kept flipping channels—mortgage payments, then CPA study plans, then whether I should redo the kitchen cabinets, and then back to money again.\n\nAnd I’d be lying in bed trying to relax, but I couldn’t really slow it down. Like I’d tell myself, okay, just pick one thing, but then another thought would jump in before I could even finish the first one.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, did they ever cause problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still getting to work and everything, so it’s not like I just stopped showing up. But I’ve made some reconciliation mistakes—enough that my supervisor pulled me aside, which was really embarrassing because numbers are supposed to be the thing I’m good at.\n\nAnd with people, it’s more like I kind of disappear. Like, I was doing salsa class for a while—my friend talked me into it, and I’m not even that coordinated, but it was fun—and lately during those periods I’ve been avoiding that and not really seeing friends. I just don’t feel like myself, and then explaining that feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: When these unusually up or irritable periods happen, do they last for at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… I think so, yeah, but it’s kind of been messy to track. The really obvious back-and-forth, like being all charged up and then crashing, has been happening for about a month now—basically since the mortgage became real.\n\nI keep tying it to that because everything got so… official, with the paperwork and numbers and thinking about payments, and then I’d be up in my head about it for stretches. So, yes, it feels like more than just a day or two, but I’d have to look back to say exactly how many days in a row.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve never been hospitalized for it. The closest thing is just feeling like everything is getting away from me, but I haven’t gone to a hospital or had anyone take me in or anything like that.\n\nHospitals make me think of when my cousin had her baby and the parking was a nightmare—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But no, not for those up periods.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in one of those up periods, do other people notice that you’re acting differently than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sister noticed. She said I was talking really fast—like I was jumping from home equity to taxes to paint colors, all in one breath—and she couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nI remember feeling it too, like I knew I was kind of steamrolling her, but I also felt this pressure to keep going because if I stopped I’d lose the thread. Which is funny because she’s usually the one who talks a lot, especially about her kitchen remodel—sorry, not the point. But yes, she definitely noticed I wasn’t talking like my usual self.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, did the symptoms ever get so severe that you couldn’t function at work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Not completely like I can’t function, no… I’m still getting to work. But it’s definitely been affecting things. I’ve made enough reconciliation mistakes that my supervisor pulled me aside, which was embarrassing because that’s kind of my whole job, you know?\n\nAnd at home or just life stuff, I haven’t really been keeping up socially. I’ve been avoiding salsa class and my friends because I don’t feel like myself. Salsa used to be the one thing where I could just go and not think about spreadsheets or mortgage numbers—well, except there’s always someone there talking about buying a condo lately, which somehow follows me everywhere. But yeah, I’m functioning, just not well.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had a period lasting at least 2 weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Lately I wake up and it’s like this heavy, empty feeling is already there before I even get out of bed. Like I’ll be sitting in my condo—the one I worked so hard to buy, and everyone said I should be proud of it—and instead I just feel like I want to cry.\n\nIt’s weird because I used to think having my own place would make me feel settled, but sometimes I’m just looking at the walls or the mortgage paperwork on the counter and it feels… hollow. Sorry, I’m getting off track. But yes, it’s been most days, and it’s not just a quick bad mood.\n\nDoctor: During that depressed period, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I stopped going to salsa class, which is weird for me because that used to be fun, like the one thing I’d actually look forward to.\n\nAnd my paints are still sitting out on the table at home—I left them there thinking I’d pick it back up, but I just look at them and feel nothing. Like they belong to somebody else. I keep meaning to put them away, but then I start thinking about how the table doesn’t even really fit in that corner, and I should’ve measured better before I bought it… sorry. But yes, I’ve lost interest in things I usually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually up periods, have you ever had hallucinations or beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there. And I don’t think I believed anything that other people were like, “That’s not real.”\n\nI mean, I’ve gotten really sure about things in my head before—like convinced I could organize my whole budget perfectly in one night or fix all my work spreadsheets if I just stayed on a roll—but that’s not the same thing, right? Sorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I’ve had hallucinations or beliefs people said weren’t true.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
